@@3003770 <p> A seminal event occurred at the start of 2015 , and hardly anyone noticed : the death of the incandescent light bulb . <p> Well , it is not quite dead , but as of Jan. 1 , 60- and 40-watt incandescent light bulbs can no longer be manufactured or imported into Canada , an extension of the ban on 75- and 100-watt bulbs that came into effect a year earlier . These were moves made by the federal government to cut energy consumption . <p> Ikea Canada president Stefan Sj ? strand says the vast majority of his own furniture comes from Ikea . Sj ? strand tells the story of when he was recognized in a Toronto Ikea store . CP Video <h> Video <p> Miles of pipe ready to become part of the Keystone Pipeline are stacked in a field near Ripley , Okla . The pipeline battle is just the start of President Barack Obama 's fight with Congress on climate change . AP <h> Video <p> While existing inventories of the bulbs will continue to be sold over the next few months @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- this is the end for the basic bulb that was invented by Thomas Edison more than a century ago . <p> Last year , many buyers stockpiled the 100-watt bulbs as they faded out of existence , but this year the transition has been more low key , retailers say . Most consumers have come to terms with the change , and the continuing drop in price of LED ( light emitting diode ) replacements has made the switch to that technology far more palatable . <p> Consequently , the rush to stock up on 40- and 60-watt bulbs has been much more muted . <p> " Last year there was quite a spike , but this year we have seen very little of a rush at all , " said Ron Cleary , senior merchant for electrical products at Home Depot Canada Inc . <p> One factor is the wide availability of halogen incandescent bulbs , a transitional technology that is similar to the old incandescents , but more efficient . These bulbs use 28 per cent less power than the old bulbs -- not nearly as much of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ CFLs ) -- but just enough of a reduction to meet federal standards . They are more expensive than the old-style incandescent bulbs , although not as costly as CFLs or LEDs . <p> But it is the lower price and wider acceptance of LEDs that is really making a difference , Mr. Cleary said . These bulbs use 90 per cent less power and last 25 times as long -- as much as 25,000 hours versus 1,000 for incandescents . Five years ago an LED bulb cost $50-$60 , he said . Now some are under $10 , and prices will continue to fall . " Over the past 12 months consumers have shown they are absolutely comfortable in changing to LEDs , " Mr. Cleary said . <p> Canadians spend about $300-million a year on light bulbs . <p> At Canadian Tire , there have also been far fewer customers stocking up on the old incandescent bulbs , said Corey Halyk , the chain 's category business manager for light bulbs . He too said the fall in LED prices is key . " They have come down to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " The customers know now that the bulb essentially pays for itself over the life of the bulb . " <p> There is now also a much broader variety of LEDs , and many are similar in shape and colour to old incandescent bulbs . They can also be dimmed , unlike most CFLs . <p> People who ca n't let go of the old bulbs will still have a few options . " Rough service " incandescent bulbs -- more expensive heavy-duty 40- and 60- watt bulbs -- will still be available . And incandescent bulbs for decorative lamps , three-way fixtures , ovens and refrigerators will still be sold . <p> And the inventory of old 40- and 60-watt bulbs will be around for a few months . Mr. Halyk said Canadian Tire stores will have some 40s and 60s until around the end of March . <p> Sales of compact fluorescents are likely to slow down , the retailers say , since they are no longer dramatically cheaper than LEDs . There is also continuing concern over CFL 's mercury content , which can spill or leach into @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , including Canadian Tire , Rona and Ikea will take back spent CFLs and recycle them . <p> Indeed , environmental concern over CFLs was one reason for a backlash against the government 's initial plans to phase out incandescent light bulbs . One Conservative federal MP , Cheryl Gallant , lobbied hard to have the government cancel the phase-out of incandescents , and organized an online petition . She appears to have given up the fight . <p> In the United States , the opposition to a similar ban -- which went into effect ahead of Canada 's -- was even more fierce . Opponents characterized the legislation as an assault on individual freedoms , and said the ban was a conspiracy among big government , lighting manufacturers and environmentalists . <h> Restrictions <p> All rights reserved . @@3003791 <p> Eleanor King 's floor-to-ceiling painting of the New York-to-Nova Scotia coastline fills the entire fourth floor gallery at the Art Gallery of Nova Scotia , and is named for an oil spill . <p> Deepwater Horizon , 2015 , is both beautiful and alarming , as King immerses viewers in the ocean and makes them look up toward the ceiling at the coastline . <p> The viewer thinks of both the Maritimes ' historical connection to the sea -- of fishing and schooner racing and the Golden Age of Sail -- and the present-day industries of extracting oil and gas . <p> BP , which owned the Deepwater Horizon rig that exploded and sank , leading to the massive 2010 oil spill in the Gulf of Mexico , paid $1 billion in 2012 to acquire the right to search for oil off the coast of Nova Scotia . It plans to drill its first exploratory well in 2017 , according to novascotia.ca . <p> King is so concerned about fossil fuel consumption and climate change that she dislikes flying . In her earlier work , she explored @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and disposable products like coffee cups . <p> The Nova Scotia artist , the 2010 Sobey Art Award finalist for Atlantic Canada and a drummer in Wet Denim , examines the dark and the light , specifically in the relationship of human beings to nature on this compromised planet , in her solo exhibit , Eleanor King : Dark Utopian , on view to June 15 . <p> The idea of dark utopia , which is the name of her song playing in the gallery , is a state of being that is both optimistic and pessimistic . <p> " When you 're looking at the news and see all sorts of injustices in the world , and when you look at climate change , which is bearing down upon us , it 's easy to feel overwhelmed , but I want to be able to imagine an alternative reality , a world I want to live in , " says King . <p> " If we do n't have anything to work toward , what are we doing here on Earth ? I have to think about a better place @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ do things that can push for that . I think that 's everyone 's responsibility . " <p> With assistants Sherry Lynn Jollimore and James Norton , the artist spent an entire week painting the gallery , working through the night till 6 a.m. the day of the opening . <p> The concept of labour and repetition is important to her art . <p> " I think a lot about labour and where our things come from , and we have very little relationship to the objects we have in our lives . <p> " There are all kinds of people in the world slaving away so we can have phones and cheap clothes . I ca n't change that , but ( this ) is a weird gesture of solidarity . " <p> Now a graduate student in New York City , King used Google Earth satellite images to depict the coastline . <p> " It 's what it would be like to sail from New York to Nova Scotia , " she says . <p> Always interested in dichotomies , King used the technologically derived images to juxtapose " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . " <p> Her vivid blue and green patterns on the walls are influenced by minimalism , hard-edge abstraction and the dazzle painting technique used in the First World War to camouflage ships . <p> " I think of the First World War as the beginning of the global war that we 're still in . It set the stage for where we still are . " <p> In 2009 at the gallery , King exhibited Obso-less-sense , a room of sculptures that were stacks of VCRs , tuners , televisions and monitors and a massive , snaking stack of discarded Tim Hortons cups . <p> " There was no way ethically to travel that show and share it in any other place , " says King , who has the Tim Hortons cups in storage . <p> " What am I going to do ? Ship a bunch of used electronics to , say , Lethbridge ? <p> " I love making physical objects and sculpture , but there is always the issue of what happens after the show . <p> " This is a way to make an immersive @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ King grew up in Hubbards so the sea is in her blood . Two overturned , functionless and oar-less wooden boats in her exhibit are borrowed from actor John Dunsworth 's yard . <p> " That 's what I love about this place . You can find things by word of mouth . " <p> Called Rafts , the boats are " sort of a lifeboat , but they are not very seaworthy . " <p> This exhibit , curated by David Diviney , the gallery 's curator of exhibitions , also includes her new Wormhole series of drawings that are created using a CD and coloured pencils and are reminiscent of Spirograph drawings . <p> " I wanted to speak of repetition and commodification without having the objects here , " says King . <p> The title refers to the wormholes that are a break in the space-time continuum , the earthworm " that eats garbage and poops out dirt " and " my ability to go through a wormhole in my mind -- that 's how I do research . " <p> The artist is a Fulbright scholar this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as a way to exhibit an immersive space without painting an entire gallery -- at the State University of New York 's Purchase College . <p> She loves living in New York . <p> " It 's been so exciting to be there . I get to see all the art I 'd normally only be reading about . It 's inspiring . " 
@@3005725 <h> The Liberal party leader on how he would translate his values into policies he 'd champion as prime minister . <p> Liberal Leader Justin Trudeau spoke at length about his values in an hour-long interview with Metro on Monday and had answers to some , but not all , of our questions on how he would put those values into policies he 'd champion as prime minister . <p> On youth and the middle class ... <p> Trudeau said he knows that many young Canadians feel prosperity is passing them by ; that their generation will not have a home , a pension , an education without crippling debt , good employment prospects and economic security upon which to start a family of their own . <p> " Young people are feeling that , " he said , " and their parents are feeling that this is a breakdown in the idea of Canada , that every generation is supposed to have greater opportunities and be able to get a better quality of life than the previous generation . " <p> That breakdown is a source of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ said . <p> His plan to solve it starts with investing in education : better bursaries and loans , looking into tying the repayment of student loans to income , and allowing Canadians to use RESPs to continue their education throughout their careers . <p> Improving the middle class , according to Trudeau , means improving the infrastructure it relies on , particularly transit , as well as encouraging both a " resource and knowledge economy . " <p> Closing the wealth gap " means supporting middle class Canadians , not supporting the wealthy . That means we 're competitive in terms of corporate taxes but not creating giveaways in hopes that corporations will create jobs . " <p> On the environment ... <p> Trudeau criticized Prime Minister Stephen Harper 's recent talk on climate change , suggesting his words were prompted by the election year and not " some kind of road to Damascus " realization that climate change exists . <p> Trudeau 's position on climate change is to support the provinces as they create systems for putting a price on carbon : Alberta has a levy , B.C. @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . " <p> On legalizing marijuana ... <p> Trudeau said his commitment to legalizing marijuana comes from his own values related to personal freedom and protecting the vulnerable , not political strategy . <p> " It 's all about allowing adults to make their choices while protecting kids much better than we are now , " he said . <p> He cited a study ( by UNICEF ) that found Canada had the highest rate of underage marijuana use among the 29 nations studied . <p> " Our current approach is n't protecting our kids , we need to protect the vulnerable , while respecting people 's freedoms , " he said . <p> On cities ... <p> In nearly 150 years since confederation , Canada has shifted from 80 per cent rural and 20 per cent urban , to the opposite and it 's time to recognize that cities deliver a majority of services on a fraction of the tax base , Trudeau said . <p> His party is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ gas tax -- that empowers cities to build infrastructure , he said . <p> Trudeau said it 's time to stop finger pointing and recognize that all governments serve the same citizens , so as prime minister , he 'd meet with the premiers and the country 's mayors and aboriginal leaders , to help them build their long-term plans . <p> " I know we 're a federation , we 're not always going to get along , but we do share a vision about what is going to be needed and I know we can work better , much more efficiently on delivering those needs instead of just saying , ' Hey , Ottawa 's balanced its books , now it 's up to Toronto or Ontario to do it on their own . This will provide McGill with a basis to proceed with its investments in a more socially responsible way . <p> Updated last May , CAMSR 's terms of reference now mandate it to proactively meet at least once a year to make recommendations to the BoG on its investments . " <p> According to McGill 's Secretary-General Stephen Strople , whose office was responsible for spearheading the application process , the committee " did a scope of the study , what it actually was that we wanted the study to do , and then we met with the director of the Office of Sustainability to talk about whether the SPF would be open to funding it . @@3005907 <h> External Links <p> ( Note : CBC does not endorse and is not responsible for the content of external links . ) <p> A Minnesota family is in Thunder Bay on Monday to share a story of life on a sailboat while studying climate change . <p> Katya Gordon and her husband run Amicus Adventure Sailing , a charter sailing business based in Knife River , Minnesota . They also do community presentations , combining their personal observations with climate change research collected from universities . <p> It 's a big change from the first time they sailed the big lake in May -- over a decade ago . <p> " I remember resolving , ' I will never go sailing in May on Lake Superior again ' ... and just the change we 've observed from then until now is almost beyond what we would have imagined , " she said . <p> " So we 've seen the sailing season markedly grow on either end . " <p> In recent years , the family has gone sailing on Superior as early as April . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " Sea Change , " a program that offers college students the chance to sail Lake Superior with them , while they study climate change . They also do community presentations . <p> " We knew we loved to sail , we knew we loved to take young people sailing , and we just felt a very strong moral pull to do something about climate change , " Gordon said . <p> The family members are not scientists and have no formal credentials to study climate change . But Gordon said they 're building relationships with scientists at various universities and they merge their personal observations with data they collect from those universities . <p> " We 're just regular citizens , so we get our information the same way as everybody else , and what we 're learning is that there is a lot of really good information out there if you know how to get it , " Gordon said . <p> She added she hopes their presentations inspire people to embrace climate change solutions . <p> The talk will be held between 12:30 and 1:30 on Monday at @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The faculty of Natural Resources Management is hosting the event and they will be sticking around in the Study coffee house on campus to talk the public from 1:30 to 3 p.m . <p> Note : The CBC does not necessarily endorse any of the views posted . And they 've given a top-up to the tanks of the anti-CBC machine . <p> The hits just keep on coming . <p> There was the Jian Ghomeshi disaster , which began with the routine CBC response : It knew nothing . Oh , yes , at the time she also had a romantic relationship with one of the directors of that company . <p> While the mismanagement and conflicts of interest were obvious , the CBC 's response to all of them was the same tired , corporate dodge for which it routinely criticizes others . <p> First deny , and , if that does n't work , then introduce some small controls and , if that does n't work , make another policy change or toss some flunky overboard . Rinse , repeat . <p> Being open , honest and ethical does n't appear to be on its checklist . The CBC talks a good game . It argues for transparency from governments and others it covers . Yet the corporation itself is about as transparent as a block of wood . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ bumbling by simply following its own rhetoric , which it actually has written down . She also attacked a CBC investigative story about the Royal Bank of Canada 's plans to replace Canadian employees with foreign workers . " <p> CBC managers danced on the head of a pin for weeks before deciding their steadfast defence was inoperable -- such payments were now banned . The managers ' conversion on the road to Damascus did n't seem @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the saddest results of these , other CBC misdeeds and the endless smokescreens generated by its sovereigns , is it casts a dark shadow over all the fine work done by the corporation . @@3006054 <h> Rare lichen discovered on P.E.I. <p> A recent survey of lichens on Prince Edward Island found a specimen that has been collected in North America just a handful of times . <p> Lichens generally grow on rocks and trees , and are a combination of two different organisms : a fungus and an algae working together to draw nutrients directly from the air . They can be small , appearing as coloured patches , or grow into long , beard-like structures . <p> Lichens are important indicators of climate change , says Troy McMullin . ( University of Guelph ) <p> " We added 66 new species that had not been recorded from the Island previously , " said Troy McMullin , a lichenologist from the University of Guelph . <p> " One of them was a species Sclerophora amabilis that 's been collected , it looks to me , about seven times in North America , ever . They came from an area that was along the Percival River , which is probably the most undeveloped region in P.E.I. " <p> McMullin compares lichens to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , lichens would be more likely to die off . That 's why it 's important , he said , to catalogue lichens as a base for tracking climate change . He wants to return to P.E.I. to look for new species of lichen . <p> Note : The CBC does not necessarily endorse any of the views posted . @@3008629 <h> Related Stories <p> The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency has urged the State Department to " revisit " how much the Keystone XL oil pipeline would contribute to global warming , saying oilsands crude produces 17 per cent more emissions than " average " crude oil . <p> In a supplemental environmental impact statement released Tuesday , the EPA suggests that the falling price of oil , now at about $52 US a barrel , should change the assessment of Keystone 's environmental impact . <p> The agency said that if the pipeline , which would ship crude oil from Canada 's oilsands to refineries along the Texas Gulf Coast , was not approved , it would be less likely that oilsands crude would find a market . <p> At oil prices in the $65 to $75 US a barrel range , " construction of the pipeline is projected to change the economics of oilsands development and result in increased oilsands production and the accompanying greenhouse gas emissions , " the EPA said in a statement . <p> ' The EPA has just reconfirmed what has been clear @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's climate test ' - Michael Brune , Sierra Club <p> A January 2014 environmental analysis by the State Department had concluded that the oilsands would be developed regardless of whether the pipeline was built . <p> " Given the recent variability in oil prices , it is important to revisit these conclusions , " wrote Cynthia Giles , assistant administrator of EPA 's enforcement office . <p> The comments come as the U.S. House of Representatives prepares to vote next week on the Keystone pipeline , following its approval in the Senate last week . <p> The bill approving the pipeline would then land on President Barack Obama 's desk . He has said he is likely to veto the bill . <h> Ammunition for Obama <p> The EPA may just have handed the president the evidence he needs to nix Keystone XL over climate change concerns . <p> The agency estimates that the pipeline would result in an additional 27. 4 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalents released into the air annually , equal to the emissions of 7.8 coal-fired plants . <p> Obama has said all along that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and that the pipeline could not exacerbate global warming . <p> Environmental groups welcomed the EPA 's statement today . <p> " The EPA has just reconfirmed what has been clear all along : the Keystone XL oilsands pipeline fails the president 's climate test , " said Michael Brune , executive director of the Sierra Club . @@3008728 <h> Invasive species pose serious threat to health of the Great Lakes <p> Found primarily in the Black Sea and the Sea of Marmara , the monkey goby is considered at a high risk of invading North American waterways . / Photo : viridiflavus <p> New study shows the vulnerability of the basin to future invaders -- and calls for regulations to mitigate this threat <p> By Melody Enguix <p> The Great Lakes have been invaded by more non-native species than any other freshwater ecosystem in the world . In spite of increasing efforts to stem the tide of invasion threats , the lakes remain vulnerable , according to scientists from McGill and colleagues in Canada and the United States . If no new regulations are enforced , they predict new waves of invasions and identify some species that could invade the Lakes over the next 50 years . <p> Over the past two centuries , more than 180 non-native species have been recorded in the Great Lakes and the rivers that flow into them . Nearly 20 per cent of these species are considered to be harmful @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ native biodiversity and multibillion dollar fishery . New threats are emerging because of risks associated with trade in live organisms and climate change , the researchers caution in a study in the Journal of Great Lakes Research . <p> Effective legislation ? <p> In recent years , the pace of species invasions appears to have slowed dramatically , as measures have been taken to protect the lakes . This is why the three scenarios ( optimistic , pessimistic and status quo ) the researchers considered for the next 50 years hinge on the implementation and effectiveness of regulations aimed at blocking current means of entry for invasive species . <p> For many years , shipping was the primary pathway for new species to enter the basin , until the rules were tightened . Ballast water is taken on board and later discharged by ships to maintain a consistent weight as they receive and offload cargo . In 2006 and 2008 , new regulations were mandated that required ships to discharge freshwater and fill their tanks with saltwater before entering the St. Lawrence Seaway . This means that ballast tank water should @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and multiplying in the Great Lakes . " No new species have been recorded since 2006 , " says Katie Pagnucco , a McGill PhD student and lead author of the study . " We may have closed the door on ballast water-mediated invasions . That remains to be seen . But other doors are still open . " <p> Future scenarios <p> In the most pessimistic scenario , greater numbers of non-native species are introduced , as ballast water regulations prove to be ineffective in the long term , and live trade continues to expand . Live trade -- the importation of live animals and plants for food markets or to be used as baitfish and aquarium pets -- is largely unregulated . Consequently , in 50 years , the Great Lakes would be populated with many new invaders , most of which may come from inland waterways where Europe and Asia meet -- the region around the Black Sea . This region is the source of some of the most disruptive invaders in the Great Lakes today , such as the zebra mussel , and still has many species @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and rivers , such as the killer shrimp or the monkey goby . <p> The status-quo scenario includes no additional protection policies . Ballast water regulations remain effective , and no new species arrive with overseas ships . The primary cause of invasions in this scenario becomes live trade . The main threat would be Asian carp species , which are well adapted to temperature conditions in much of the Great Lakes . They are voracious plankton-feeders , capable of diverting energy from the rest of the food web , and thus could impact the fishery . <p> In the most optimistic scenario , the risk of invasion is minimized by harmonized policies enforced by Canada and the United States . " Invasions are a transboundary issue , " says McGill professor Anthony Ricciardi , an invasive-species biologist who supervised the study . " In addition to harmonized regulations on live trade , the two countries must coordinate early detection and rapid response to new threats -- before an invasion has progressed beyond control . " <p> Weakened temperature barrier <p> The researchers caution that climate change will complicate each of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have already had all the invasive species from the Mississippi that could survive there , as a temperature barrier is protecting us from others " notes Pagnucco . But warmer waters will facilitate the migration and establishment of southern species , most notably via the canal from the Mississippi to the Great Lakes . <p> Invasion risks are dynamic and management responses must evolve to cope with them , Ricciardi and Pagnucco caution . <p> Currently you have JavaScript disabled . @@3008764 <p> Tuesday , February 3 , 2015 , 9:42 AM - Have you ever wanted to see the invisible ? The above animations , comprised of images snapped by NOAA 's GOES-13 and GOES-15 geostationary weather satellites , are showing you just that . <p> Weather is going on around our planet at all hours of the day or night . Clouds are forming and dying , storms are rumbling , rain , snow and ice are falling , skies are clearing , and the processes behind all this repeat again and again in a chaotic , random fashion . <p> Weather satellites , like those run by NOAA - the US 's National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration - capture amazing imagery of these patterns , and we 're all familiar with these views . <p> GOES 13 satellite view . Credit : NOAA-NASA GOES Project <p> However , the view presented in the video animation is one that we do n't often see , and would never see without the help of these satellites ( or some similar technology ) . The animation shows our planet in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ can not pick up , and reveals the patterns of heat radiating off the surface . <p> Since we 're really not used to seeing the invisible , it 's difficult to pick out specific features of the map , so here 's a primer to help . <p> The brightest regions of the infrared view correspond to the combination of the hottest and driest areas , thus the greatest amount of heat escaping from the surface into space for the satellite to see . The darker regions are cooler , or regions where the atmosphere is saturated with water vapour , which blocks these wavelengths of light from reaching the top of the atmosphere ( the water absorbs them ) . <p> Why is it important to look at the Earth like this ? Seeing the flow of water in the atmosphere is important for tracking storms and weather systems , but it goes beyond just looking at clouds . There are subtle variations that can tell us a lot about what 's going on with the planet , and it 's also important to see just how much water @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ water vapour is one of the strongest greenhouse gases , and as temperatures continue to climb with global warming and climate change , the amount of water vapour contained in the atmosphere is increasing as well . <p> Is all this water vapour responsible for global warming ? To a small degree , yes , but only due to the presence of other greenhouse gases , like carbon dioxide . This is due to the fact that the warming effects of water vapour are not self-sustaining . That is , when high concentrations of water vapour ( a ' condensing greenhouse gas ' ) are present in an area , this not only traps infrared radiation , but it produces clouds - a ' negative feedback ' which block sunlight and has a cooling effect . Thus , while water vapour does have a strong effect on the temperatures in our atmosphere , it really only amplifies or diminishes the heating from other effects . <p> On the other hand , the warming effects of carbon dioxide - a ' non-condensing greenhouse gas ' - are self-sustaining , because higher concentrations @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with water . There 's only more warming . <p> Given the fact that CO2 makes up about 80 per cent of the non-condensing greenhouse gases in the atmosphere ( along with methane , ozone and a few others ) , this gas is very important in regulating the temperature of the planet , and according to NASA , " atmospheric carbon dioxide therefore qualifies as the principal control knob that governs the temperature of Earth . " <p> Studies of the greenhouse effect have shown that if all carbon dioxide and other non-condensing GHGs currently in the atmosphere were removed , the average surface temperature of the planet would plummet from 15oC to nearly -20oC within 25 years . This is despite CO2 and these other GHGs being ' trace gases ' , amounting to less than 0.04 per cent of the total volume and mass of the air surrounding Earth . <p> This is why there is so much concern about the amount of carbon dioxide and methane being added to the atmosphere by human activity . <p> Small amounts add up to so much of an effect already @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the hundredths of a percent of the total atmosphere , is going to have big consequences .

@@3011880 <h> After so long , is the acid rain issue an environmental success story ? Well , sort of ... <p> Back in the ' 70s , ' 80s and ' 90s , when the world was mobilizing to heal the damaged ozone layer , and the dire warnings about global climate change were really just starting , acid rain was making ominous headlines in the news . These days , we rarely hear anything about it , so what ever happened to this issue ? Is it over ? Did we solve that one ? <p> Well , sort of ... <p> Acid rain is caused when gases like carbon dioxide , nitrogen dioxide and sulphur dioxide - which can be naturally-occurring but are also released by burning fossil fuels like coal and oil - dissolve in water vapour and water droplets in clouds . <p> When combined with water , these gases ( either directly or through a sequence of reactions ) form into carbonic acid , nitric acid and sulphuric acid , respectively , which fall with the rain and end up in groundwater , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rainwater can be ever-so-slightly acidic . Read on the pH scale ( presented to the right ) rainwater can come in anywhere between 5.5 and 6.5 . This is generally fairly harmless to life and nature has ways of dealing with these slightly acidic levels ( both biologically , and geologically - like through the dissolving of limestone , for example ) . <p> However , if the pH drops to less than 5.5 , nature ca n't deal with these levels as effectively . The chemistry of waterways changes . Plants and trees are damaged , producing ' burned out ' forests . Fish eggs wo n't hatch . Insect species die off . Entire aquatic ecosystems can be compromised , resulting in ' dead lakes ' . In some cities , statues and buildings made of limestone and marble are being eaten away by the corrosive rain . <p> In Canada , nowhere were the effects of acid rain more severe and widespread than in the vicinity of Sudbury , Ont. , due to the sulphur emissions from the nickel smelting operations there . <p> " Sudbury is often @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Smol , the Canada Research Chair in Environmental Change at Queen 's University , in Kingston , Ont . <p> With the sulphur emissions depositing in the local environment , and the Canadian Shield 's lack of any significant limestone buffer , this spelled doom for much of the plant life in the region ( save those that could tolerate the acidic soils , like blueberry bushes and trees such as the paper birch ) . <p> With the need for action great , the United States and Canada stepped up to impose limits on the amount of sulphur dioxide and nitrogen dioxide being released into the atmosphere by industry and power generation plants . <p> How did these measures do ? As of 2007 , the EPA reported that - through the use of a cap-and-trade system - not only had the United States reached their emission targets at least three years ahead of schedule , but they 'd done so even more cheaply than expected , with costs coming in at roughly one-quarter what they were originally projected . Environment Canada reports showed similar progress and successes with the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 
@@3014746 <h> Getting rid of petroleum stocks is a crucial first step for universities Add to ... <p> Kathryn Harrison , George Hoberg , and David Tindall are faculty members in , respectively , Political Science , Forest Resources Management and Sociology at the University of British Columbia . <p> Faculty members at the University of British Columbia are voting this week on a resolution calling on the university to divest its endowment from fossil-fuel stocks over a period of five years . UBC students voted overwhelmingly in favour of divestment last year . <p> In our experience , critics of divestment by universities and other public institutions typically offer three arguments : That moral principles have no place in institutional investments ; that those who advocate divestment are naive , or even hypocritical , because they rely on fossil fuels themselves ; and that the focus on fossil-fuel producers is misplaced because they are only serving consumer demand . We have considered and reject each of these three points . <p> First , it 's true that the divestment movement is predicated on a moral argument . Our @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ their future -- to be funded by profits from industries whose activities are inherently detrimental to that future . The fact that others might be happy to consume or profit from fossil fuels does not detract from that principled position . <p> Some have argued that while individuals are free to invest their own money according to their principles , public institutions must seek only to maximize returns . We disagree . Universities , churches , and governments make moral decisions on behalf of their communities all the time . We see no reason that investments should be any different . Nor should the status quo be exempt from moral scrutiny . That universities currently seek to profit from fossil fuels is not morally neutral . It is a choice to support activities that their own researchers have concluded are antithetical to ecological sustainability , human welfare and social justice . <p> Second , it is true that those who call for divestment rely on fossil fuels in their own lives . It is impossible to avoid reliance on fossil fuels in today 's economy , however committed one is to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that by living in the modern world one forgoes the right to call for change is to dismiss out of hand almost all possibilities for social change . <p> Third , it 's often argued that divestment simply wo n't work , because today 's consumers will continue to demand fossil fuels . But that can and must change . Just as tobacco companies worked feverishly to challenge the scientific conclusion that cigarettes cause cancer and to thwart public policies to deter smoking , so too have fossil-fuel companies led the charge against equally certain climate science and policies to reduce greenhouse-gas emissions . Divestment challenges those firms ' social license , just as anti-smoking campaigns did for the tobacco industry . It alerts individuals , as both consumers and citizens , to the impact of their decisions . <p> That is critical because , at the end of the day , there is no question that divestment is a second-best solution . Government action , ideally via a national carbon price , is the preferred response to climate change . <p> Unfortunately , government leadership on climate change has been @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ national climate plans over 25 years , none of which has even come close to meeting its targets . In the meantime , Canada 's emissions have increased steadily . Policies have been promised but not implemented , a trend exemplified by the federal government 's recent abandonment of its commitment to regulate emissions from oil production -- a sector that accounts for most of Canada 's emissions growth . <p> Today 's youth are understandably impatient with this leadership vacuum . In creating the divestment movement they are also facilitating the preferred solution of government action . In mobilizing principled rejection of an unsustainable industry , divestment helps to undermine the political influence of fossil-fuel companies and thus to create greater political space for elected leaders to adopt much-needed policies to reduce our reliance on fossil fuels . <p> The divestment movement has emerged as a powerful voice for our students ' generation , demanding , justifiably , that their universities , markets and ultimately our elected representatives do better . We stand with them .

In 2014 , TerraTherm designed , constructed , and began operation of the largest in situ thermal remediation project ever attempted in the world , at the former Williams Air Force Base in Mesa , Arizona . This Steam Enhanced Extraction ( SEE ) system is treating a subsurface volume of 410,000 cubic yards , containing up to 2,000,000 gallons of jet fuel , to a depth of 245 feet below ground surface . <p> TerraTherm was also recognized for its role in delivering and implementing the In Situ Thermal Desorption ( ISTD ) remedial solution at the Teterboro Landing site , covering an area of 3.2 acres with a treated volume of 122,000 cubic yards of contaminated soil . The cleanup at the site , which operated for less than eight months and was completed in 2014 , constituted the largest ISTD project ever completed . @@3017932 <p> It will take time , as it always does , for history to cast its verdict on the diplomatic career of Canada 's departed foreign minister John Baird . For now , it 's likely safe to say -- if those who 've worked the vineyards of diplomacy are correct -- that few orders for statues are being placed . <p> Canada 's foreign policy under Prime Minister Stephen Harper has been , in the view of former ambassador to the United Nations Paul Heinbecker , marked by " pinched vision , shrunken ambition and political cynicism masquerading as principle . " <p> As Baird , who manned the portfolio since 2011 , sets off into the political afterlife of ministering to connections and making money , and with a federal election imminent and Canada 's 150th birthday on the horizon in 2017 , it might be time for some national reflection on the country 's role in the world . <p> Plenty of voices say Canada 's international standing is far from what it once was and nowhere close to what it could and should be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ institutions , regional turbulence and Islamist extremism . <p> Heinbecker told author Michael Harris , for his recent book Party of One : Stephen Harper and Canada 's Radical Makeover , that Canadian diplomacy is hugely different under Harper . <p> " We used to be advocates of constructive internationalism . . . That 's why our advice and our particular insight were so sought after by other countries . <p> " Now , we are a country with baggage . " <p> The Harper government " has aggressively narrowed Canada 's official international policy to concentrate on trade and military initiatives , diluting Canada 's signature contributions to both international development and multinationalism , most notably in the tinderbox of the Middle East , " Clark said in his 2013 book How We Lead : Canada in a Century of Change . <p> For his part , Baird once explained @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ We 're not a referee . " The reasons for change should be stated clearly , and discussed , and justified in open debate . That has not been how the Harper government conducts its foreign policy . Only after World War I did we come out from behind British skirts and take responsibility for our affairs abroad . Until Mackenzie King appointed Louis St. Laurent as secretary of state for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ task on the prime minister 's plate . <p> It remains the case , Heinbecker observed , that " Prime Ministers make foreign policy . " But foreign ministers can determine whether it succeeds , and some make lasting marks . <p> Canada 's performance in two world wars built international prestige . Canadians excelled in helping construct a new international order after WWII , and it was soon often said that the country was " punching above its weight . " The Nobel Prize won by Lester Pearson for his role in the Suez Crisis of 1956 was a signal 20th-century moment for the country . <p> Canada " helped breathe life , " Heinbecker said , into the United Nations and helped create NATO , the International Monetary Fund , the World Bank , the Colombo Plan for development plan for former British colonies , the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade . <p> After Pearson , two prime ministers played large roles abroad . Pierre Trudeau emphasized a better deal for the world 's mushrooming poor . In the ' 80s and ' 90s , Brian Mulroney 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ capitals and acclaim in contributing to the abolition of apartheid . <p> The biggest foreign policy legacies of Jean Chr ? tien , a more cautious political homebody , were probably Lloyd Axworthy 's " human security agenda " ( which sought to put protecting people ahead of protecting states ) and the decision to stay out of the Iraq War ( a position much criticized then , but widely lauded now ) . <p> As Axworthy once acknowledged , foreign affairs work is not wholly born of altruism . " We win in a stable , equitable , co-operative world . We lose when it is turbulent , divisive and unfair . " <p> It was during the austerity agenda of the Chr ? tien years that Canada turned inward . " <p> In 2003 , author Andrew Cohen said in his book While Canada Slept : How We Lost Our Place in the World that " we are no longer as strong @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a diplomat , and it has diminished us as a people . " <p> Enter Stephen Harper and his newly constituted Conservative Party . <p> As Heinbecker wrote , the Harper government came to power in 2006 with scant international experience . " He had no interest in international stuff . We simply could n't get him to travel . " <p> Joe Clark wrote , moreover , that " international issues have played virtually no part " in the elections Harper has won . <p> The results of all this have been baleful , he said . In 2010 , Canada failed to win election to the United Nations Security Council . <p> To Paul Heinbecker , the reasons were clear . Harper 's government " had scarcely concealed its contempt for the UN , abandoned Canada 's commitment on climate change , shifted Canada 's aid policy focus from the relatively poorer Africa to the relatively richer Americas , gave largely uncritical support to Israel 's deepening occupation of the West Bank and its blockade of Gaza , and mismanaged relationship with China " and others . <p> In all , the consensus seems to be that Baird was a loyal lieutenant . @@3018216 <h> Wind energy is viable <p> A prototype of a floating wind turbine operates in the harbour of Marseille , southern France , on Jan. 23 , 2015 , before being set offshore . The wind turbine will be tested in the Mediterranean Sea in 2016 , ahead of a planned offshore wind farm with 13 turbines in 2018 . Lower than traditional wind turbines , it requires less maintenance and works regardless of the orientation of the wind . AFP PHOTO / BORIS HORVAT/AFP/Getty Images <p> The price of electricity paid by Ontarians is rising due to decades of under-investment in both new sources of electricity and the electricity transmission infrastructure needed to deliver that power . <p> Wind energy is cheaper than new nuclear power and cost-competitive with new hydroelectric power . It is not subject to the risks of rising natural gas prices nor any future price levied on carbon emissions to address climate change . <p> Wind energy developers absorb most of the upfront risk and cost of building new wind energy projects . The result is no front-end or long-term risks to taxpayers @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and competitive . <p> Every megawatt of new wind energy represents an investment of approximately $2 million -- a large portion of which is spent in the local community , supporting new manufacturing facilities and new local jobs . <h> Comments <p> We encourage all readers to share their views on our articles and blog posts . @@3021766 <h> The high cost of ' balance ' in the vaccination debate <h> The improbable measles outbreak illustrates the dangers of debating the facts . <p> The call for debate , discussion , balance and choice serves to bogusly validate the fuzzy thinking of the " anti-vaxxers . " <p> By:John Semley Published on Sun Feb 08 2015 <p> Last week , measles hit Toronto . Measles . In 2015 , getting infected with measles strikes me as about as likely as getting pancaked by a Model T or bumping into Warren G. Harding at the movies . And yet , here we are : four adults and two children diagnosed in Toronto , another recent case reported in Niagara , and a mounting wave of confused hysteria that , yes , somehow , people are still getting measles . <p> The local measles uptick comes after an outbreak Stateside , believed to have originated in or around the Disneyland theme park in Anaheim . It 's more fuel for the fires stoking the current debate around vaccinations , and whether today 's modern parent should bother with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ against virulent diseases . <p> Students at Queen 's University have raised concerns over professor Melody Torcolacci , whose Physical Determinants of Health class floats the disproven idea that vaccinations are tied to disability and chronic illness in children ( " She is a crock , " reads one particularly scathing appraisal on the website Rate My Professor ) . Recently , New Jersey governor and potential U.S. presidential candidate Chris Christie called for " balance " in the argument about child vaccinations , stating that " parents need to have some measure of choice " in the matter . <p> Maybe it 's superfluous to claim that Christie , a man who delusionally believes that he 's a personal friend of Bruce Springsteen 's , is wrong about something but , well , Chris Christie is wrong . And Melody Torcolacci is wrong . And the 20 per cent of Ontarians who , according to a recent poll , still believe in some link between inoculation and child autism are wrong . And everyone who does n't vaccinate their children is dead wrong . <p> There is no need for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ choice . " Because believing in anti-vaccination is an indefensible position . The benefit of vaccinating yourself against infectious diseases is n't a theory or proposition or a position . It 's a fact . There 's no reason to even " debate " the issue . Debating vaccination versus anti-vaccination is like debating whether 2 + 2 = 4 or whether Hooch was the dog in Turner &amp; Hooch . Look at a graph charting reported cases of measles in the United States . It 's like looking at the first pitch of an especially terrifying roller coaster . Following the introduction of the vaccine in 1963 , cases of measles dropped from the hundreds of thousands to the tens of thousands . ( Reporting in Canada was a bit spottier for the years immediately following the vaccine 's licensing , but the general downslope remains consistent . ) <p> Debating vaccination is like debating climate change -- another verifiable fact that , according to a 2013 Guardian report , was backed by around 97 per cent of the scientific community . Similarly , the MMR vaccine that defends against @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ per cent effective , an overwhelming number . ( One of the cases reported in Toronto afflicted someone who was fully vaccinated , one of those **30;79;TOOLONG type things . ) <p> While the essence of democracy may well be lively , spirited , productive deliberation over issues of major public importance , there 's no great virtue in blowing hot air over something that can be safely taken for granted . Like , say , the truism that immunization against infectious , potentially fatal diseases is a good thing . The call for debate , discussion , consideration , balance and choice often serves to bogusly validate the seriousness of the so-called " anti-vaxxers . " Sometimes putting a notion on the proverbial table validates its specious right to even be there . It makes unserious ideas -- or moronic or dangerous ones -- seem totally vital and significant . <p> And the cost of lending some appearance of seriousness to the anti-vaxxers ' ideas is perilously steep . To achieve " herd immunity " -- the minimum level of immunization in a given population to stop the spread of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cent in the case of measles -- requires that the vast majority of us work together . When inoculations dip below that threshold , many more people are prone to get sick . Herd immunity fundamentally changes the algebra of personal " choice . " Because where the unvaccinated are protected by the vaccinated , the opposite is not true . <p> If some freethinking adult wants to live in some hovel up in the hills and slowly die of rubella then , well , that 's their prerogative . But this freedom ends where mine , or anyone else 's , freedom begins . You do n't have a right to get other people sick . <p> Likewise , while anyone technically has the right to advocate against vaccination , such dangerously wrong-headed , totally counterfactual garbage does n't automatically deserve to be accepted , or even tolerated . You ca n't contest facts , or even high probabilities . Two and two is four . Medicine is good . Hooch was the dog . 
@@3021795 <h> Related Stories <h> External Links <p> ( Note : CBC does not endorse and is not responsible for the content of external links . ) <p> People who are about to make a public announcement tend to engage in two broad types of behaviour ( as always the exceptions would fill an entire book ) . <p> They 're either among the first people to arrive and welcome others with chatter , handshaking and schmoozing , or they wait until the last moment , then walk into the room with all eyes fixed on them as they head for the podium . <p> Charlie Oliver is of the former type . You can tell right away he 's a live wire . <p> He crackles with energy -- even his thoughts have an hyper-ionized quality to them , as if permanently on a triple charger . Whether you really know him or not , he immediately treats you like his best friend . <p> Memorial University 's business school , which named him Entrepreneur of the Year in 2008 , describes him as a " key deal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ himself once boasted that his greatest strength is the ability to " dream solutions to insolvable problems , then to convince others to buy in . " <p> Ten days ago , this super-successful St. John 's mega-realtor announced he wants the whole province to buy into his latest brain child : the website thinkNL . What he 's come up with is a web version of what the ancient Greeks called their agora and the Romans their forum -- the public place , usually in the centre of their town or city , where they hung out and discussed important issues including matters of state . <h> Let 's skip the hemlock , shall we ? <p> Now , the Greeks and Romans were notorious for their philosophical and political partisanship . You had the cynics , sceptics , stoics , atomists , epicureans , neoplatonists , and last but not least the sophists , who liked to argue circles around everybody else -- all of them creating a lot of noise but very little consensus . <p> If you tried to call the bluff on the whole intellectual cacophony @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ or a draught of hemlock . <p> That 's exactly what Oliver does n't want to happen on thinkNL . He wants people to leave their partisanship at the door and explore common solutions , not differences . <p> Oliver is on to something . <p> Take the Irish , who have n't been able to discuss their common past without regurgitating it over and over like a partially digested meal , and God have mercy on the hand extended to shake on an agreement with the other side . Too many old grievances continue to obscure the view of a less divided future . <p> To end this stalemate is the mission of Arkiv , a web-based , non-partisan debate on Ireland formed in 2013 . Its members say they 're deeply unhappy with the way discussions about a common for the Irish keep tripping over the the same old baggage . <h> Looking backward , thinking forward <p> They also quote Danish philosopher Soren Kierkegaard , who famously said , " Life can only be understood backward , but it must be lived forward . " <p> Charlie Oliver @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ quality to them , as if permanently on a triple charger . <p> That 's precisely Charlie Oliver 's point when he talks about wanting to hand the province over to future generations in better shape than it is now . <p> " We can not get there if we constantly focus on the past and what has been wrong , " he said . " We need to understand the problem but focus more on the solution . " <p> Not a mean challenge considering that focusing on the past is at the very heart of the partisan game that keeps politicians in this part of the world in business . There 's no better way of biting a political opponent in the rear than with his or her own history . <p> Sometimes it seems that political ideas are nothing more than ropes with which to choke if not strangle the opposition , and that ideas which ca n't be used in such a way do n't even get raised . <h> The pit of partisanship <p> Even in the United States , where partisan politics is practised with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ second thoughts . <p> " The whole debate over climate change has never been about science , " laments science writer Christie Aschwanden in an article with the headline " Partisanship Has Swallowed Up The Debate Over Climate Change " . <p> The State of Mississippi is going one step further and contemplating concrete action by removing partisan politics from future elections altogether . As John Opdyke of the group Open Primaries says , " We need more competition in the political process , more inclusiveness , more debate and more voters . " <p> Oliver 's thinkNL does n't go quite that far , but it is a step in the same direction . <p> Removing partisan thinking from public debate may be a bit like taking gossip out of the office and salt out of food , but it does bring things back to their basics . <p> Note : The CBC does not necessarily endorse any of the views posted . @@3024066 <p> Australia 's scorching heat wave of 2013 , which triggered fierce bushfires and broke more than 100 temperature-related records , including one for the country 's hottest day ever recorded , would have been virtually impossible without climate change , a new report says . <p> " The evidence on the link between climate change and extreme heat is stronger than ever and , in fact , is overwhelming , " said the report , adding " there is a ' calculable ' human influence on the record hot summer of 2012-2013 . " <p> It also pointed out that the number of record hot days in Australia has increased strongly since 1950 and particularly sharply in the last two decades . <p> The heat wave of 2013 -- which later came to be known as the " angry summer " -- would not have happened if it had n't been for climate change , Will Steffen , a climate expert and author of the report , told The Australian Associated Press . <p> ( Steffen is a member of the Climate Council , an independent , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Tim Flannery . ) <p> Australia experienced its hottest day , month , season and even hottest calendar year in 2013 , registering a mean temperature 1.2C above the 1961-90 average . <p> The report , titled Quantifying the Strong Influence of Climate Change on Extreme Heat in Australia , said that recent studies show these heat events would have occurred only once every 12,300 years without greenhouse gas emissions from human activities . <p> The characteristics of the country 's heat waves have also changed from 1950 to 2013 , the report said : they have become hotter and are lasting longer . They are also occurring more often and starting earlier . <p> Taking data from various studies , Steffen 's report broke down what the heat wave has meant for different Australian cities : <p> In Sydney , Australia 's most populated city , heat waves now start 19 days earlier . <p> In Canberra , the number of heat-wave days has more than doubled . <p> In Hobart , heat-wave days start 12 days earlier . <p> In Melbourne , the hottest heat-wave day is 2C hotter @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> In Adelaide , the hottest heat-wave day is 4.3C hotter and the number of heat-wave days has almost doubled . <p> The 2013 Australian heat wave made headlines all over the world but it was the Holmes ' family ordeal in early January that went viral and captured the consequences of the unrelenting heat wave . <p> Tim and Tammy Holmes were babysitting their five grandchildren in the small Tasmanian fishing town of Dunally when a wildfire engulfed the town . According to multiple reports , there was no escape for the family , so they ran for the water . <p> Tim took a photograph of the family cowering in the water , with a wall of flames behind them . <p> The Climate Council report said that new research strengthens the case for strong action on climate change . In his blog , Steffen wrote that " carbon emissions must be reduced rapidly and deeply if the worst of extreme heat in the second half of the century is to be avoided . @@3026658 <p> Last week , a student-led initiative asked McGill 's board of governors to initiate a process to divest the university 's endowment fund from companies directly involved in oilsands development , and eventually from the fossil fuel industry altogether . <p> The request comes in the form of a petition to the board 's Committee to Advise on Matters of Social Responsibility , whose terms of reference explicitly include environmental harm in its definition of social injury . In this , Divest McGill joins similar initiatives underway at campuses across North America . More than 100 McGill faculty members have signed a letter of support . <p> Science shows that there is a limit to the quantity of fossil fuels that can be burned before global warming roasts us . Over the last century , we have already emitted about half of our trillion ton carbon dioxide allocation . This may seem like a lot , but if all the proven fossil fuel reserves were burned , they would emit about three times this amount . <p> Corporate social responsibility thus mandates that fossil fuel companies abandon @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to massively reinvest in carbon-free alternatives . Since the stock market value of the companies depends directly on the size of their reserves , these holdings represent a huge " carbon bubble " that will burst when climate agreements begin to limit the exploitation of known reserves . In spite of this massive overcapitalization , in 2013 fossil fuel companies collectively invested nearly $700 billion in augmenting and developing their reserves . This is nearly three times the annual global investment in carbon-free alternatives . Worse , whereas 15 years ago several of the companies made major investments in renewables , these have since been shed and for most , alternatives today represent less than a shrinking 1 per cent of their total investments . <p> In the absence of large-scale carbon capture and storage , the companies seem to be betting that global collective action to limit climate change will continue to be ineffectual , that fossil fuels will continue to be burned without limit . They actively lobby against action and support the spread of climate disinformation . <p> This behaviour is immoral and must be sanctioned . Inspired @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a new global movement has arisen with the aim of casting opprobrium on those who profit from and speculate on climate Armageddon . As in the 1970s , the movement has been spearheaded by campuses ; today there are over 600 fossil fuel divestment movements worldwide and over 180 institutions with assets of over $50 billion have pledged to divest of their fossil fuel holdings . Most recently , Glasgow and Humboldt universities have taken the decision to divest . In Canada , the University of Victoria and University of British Columbia have particularly advanced divestment movements and their boards of governors are now under strong pressure to divest . <p> The Divest McGill campaign is calling for divestment of the estimated 5 per cent of McGill 's $1 billion endowment . @@3029505 <h> Divesting from the fossil fuel industry is the morally , environmentally , and financially right thing for responsible universities to do . <p> Campaigners for divestment argue that universities have a responsibility to get rid of all investments in the fossil fuel industry . <p> By:Graham Henry Published on Wed Feb 11 2015 <p> Divesting from the fossil fuel industry is the morally , environmentally and financially right thing for responsible universities to do . Yet when the members of a university community ask ( as professors at the University of British Columbia did on Tuesday ) , quite reasonably , for their endowment to be divested from fossil fuels , a negative nose-bloodying knee jerk will undoubtedly result . Here are some common knee jerks and why they simply do n't stand up to closer examination . <p> Some critics of divestment will say fossil fuel companies are actually the biggest investors in renewable energy and only responding to public demand when they supply fossil fuels . This is simply not true . Only three of the largest fossil fuel companies spend more than 1 per @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's more , in 2012 the industry spent $647 billion seeking new reserves to burn . The only future these companies are bunkering down for is one dependent on burning oil , gas and coal . An industry that spent $213 million in 2013 ( in just the U.S. and European Union ) lobbying regulators is doing much more than simply meeting the demands of the population ; they are actively working to maintain our reliance on fossil fuels . With what we now know about climate change , why should universities support this ? <p> Others will say a university endowment must invest based solely on securing the best financial returns . Ironically , even if this were true , there is little reason to cling desperately to this industry . The fossil fuel age is on the way out . It has to be . Investors are realizing this and fossil fuel stocks are no longer the surefire investments they once were . <p> In fact , if the University of Toronto had divested from fossil fuels 10 years ago , it would be sitting on a larger endowment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ oil prices and the evaporating value of high intensity extraction such as tar sands , the optimism of recent years seems like a distant memory . <p> The idea that a university should only care about financial returns is also wrong both technically and morally . The University of Toronto , for example , has a divestment policy that says " in specific instances where the University 's social responsibility as an investor is questioned " they must respond by considering " the injurious impact which the activities of a company are found to have on consumers , employees , or other persons . " <p> The injurious impacts of climate change are undeniable and escalating . Universities are much more than financial institutions and that is a very good thing : they are inspiring places built on the idea that the knowledge we learn can be used to better both ourselves and the world at large . They are institutions perfectly poised to lead our country boldly into the future and moral actions based on fact should undeniably be part of that vision . <p> Holding stocks in fossil fuel @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , sell a lot of carbon , make a lot of money , and that nothing will get in the way of these things happening . You are betting against effective action on greenhouse gas emissions . This is not a bet that any responsible university can continue to make . <p> As university students we do n't have much in the way of social capital to help us change the world . We ride our bicycles , read our books , suffer through exams , and do n't typically run corporations , draft laws or speak to people through their TVs . <p> Instead , what we have is a collective voice as the upcoming generation . Fossil fuel divestment is an entirely responsible move on climate change spurred by a generation not nearly as naive as some would have you believe . We are ready to act on climate change . If you 're not willing to lead , we are ready to make the first move . The time to divest is now . <p> Graham Henry is a student at University Toronto Faculty of Law , Treasurer @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ UofT . Friday , Feb. 13 , is Global Divestment Day of Action . www.350.org

@@3029550 <h> How About a Memorial tothe Victims of Harperism ? <h> J. F. Conway <p> " Only a fool would deny that millions have been the tragic victims of communism , but that number pales , surely , in comparison with the victims of capitalism . " <p> -- Roy MacGregor , Globe and Mail , 31 January 2015 . <p> Roy MacGregor was commenting on Harper 's latest ideological propaganda foray into keeping us safe by reminding us of all the bogeymen out there on the very big , broad left . ( Harper 's definition of communism is really quite loose , just look at some of his past comments on Pierre Trudeau . ) Harper is building a big ugly memorial to the victims of communism on a million dollar piece of public land right beside the Supreme Court . ( Could this be revenge for their decisions dismantling key pieces of his agenda ? ) And it is ugly , really ugly -- just look at the sketches of the proposed memorial . It is right up there with the ugliest of Stalinist politically @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ its implosion in 1991 . <p> Stephen Harper would be a rather sad and pathetic figure if he did n't hold the power of the Canadian state firmly in his grasp . This allows him to impose his folksy fanaticism on the rest of us . He is without doubt the most fierce right-wing ideologue to occupy the office of prime minister since R.B. Bennett , who in 1919 vowed to crush communism " under the iron heel of ruthlessness . " And he proceeded to do so . But Bennett had some real , tough class enemies to deal with in the days of the Winnipeg General Strike and the Great Depression . Alas , poor Stephen , born so long after his time . Today there are no dangerous communists in Canada to crush . All our prime minister has to crush are " lone-wolf , home-grown , and radicalized " terrorists : psychologically deranged individuals who tie their delusional system to mad , suicidal acts of targeted revenge at the behest of Islamic State propagandists . <p> The elephant in the room no one talks about , of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ IS forces and territory in Iraq . But erase the thought from your mind these " lone-wolf " attacks might be twisted , demented blowback from our military strikes . That is dangerous thinking . Soon there will be a new Harper law making such thoughts a crime , since it could be seen as somehow justifying terrorist acts . In Harperland explaining is justifying . Be safe , just denounce . <p> The public fears Harper is orchestrating over these " lone-wolf " terrorist " threats to our way of life " hardly measure up to the radical class-struggle syndicalism of workers in 1919 and in the 1930s , or the growing communist parties across the capitalist world in the wake of the 1917 Bolshevik Revolution . These were real , palpable threats to the capitalist system . So poor Stephen is reduced to building a memorial to the enemy he wishes he could have been there to crush . His life-long obsession with the battle against communism , which curiously he now invokes against that capitalist gangster Vladimir Putin over the conflict in Ukraine , does n't allow him @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ memorials to the victims of communism in the world . In 2002 such a memorial was unveiled in Prague , focusing on political prisoners . Artistically , it is actually quite moving . Not to be outdone in exhibiting his anti-communist credentials , in 2007 President George W. Bush dedicated a memorial in Washington , D.C. It is a rather modest piece -- a three-meter tall bronze rendering of the Goddess of Democracy built by Chinese students in Tiananmen Square in 1989 . <p> Harper makes up for this lack of originality by ensuring Canada 's memorial will be very large and very expensive . The land is prime land smack dab between the Supreme Court and the National Library . The land is worth $1-million and the memorial will cost an estimated $5-million . Harper , on our behalf , has donated the land and $3-million . A charitable trust , the Tribute to Liberty , will raise the rest . And do n't worry if you donate and get a tax receipt . There is no way that Harper 's Canada Revenue Agency will take away the trust 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not ! ) Harper has donated the very land earlier plans had ear marked for a new Pierre Trudeau Judicial Building . We can all be proud . Canada will have the biggest , most expensive , and by far the ugliest memorial to the victims of communism in the world . Another first for Harper 's Canada . <h> Victims of Capitalism <p> MacGregor is correct to note the number of victims of communism " pales " in comparison to those of capitalism . A full account of the victims of capitalism would stretch back at least four centuries . The number of victims is incalculably vast . Just off the top of my head , we would have to include , completely ignoring the long battle to undermine and overthrow feudalism , the following : the millions of blacks taken from Africa in the slave trade ( and the slaughter of those who resisted capture and those who died en route ) ; the vicious genocides committed against the indigenous peoples of Australia , New Zealand , North and South America , and the Caribbean Islands ; the savage @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ capitalist powers fighting for resources , treasure , labour and markets ; the English Civil War ; the French Revolution ; the Napoleonic wars ; the European revolutions of the 1830s and 1840s ; the American Civil War ; the incessant wars among the great powers culminating in World Wars I and II . Need I go on ? Let us not have a memorial to the victims of capitalism . It would be too immense and too expensive . It would also provoke despair as we gazed upon it , contemplating the human costs exacted by the movement of history . <p> Let 's be more modest . After we defeat Harper let 's build a memorial to the victims of Harperism . The numbers are far more manageable , immediate and easier to grasp . Who would be included on the list ? The young men and women sent to kill and die in a senseless war in Afghanistan ( and those who may yet die fighting IS in Iraq ) . The returned psychologically and physically wounded veterans , and the many who , despondent , have committed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ help . We should not forget the prisoners who have committed suicide or retreated into madness after endless incarceration in solitary confinement . The murdered and missing aboriginal women , a systemic problem Harper chooses to ignore . The many aboriginals who live in soul-destroying conditions without adequate water , sewage disposal , safe housing , or affordable food , and witness an epidemic of suicide among the hopeless young in their communities . How can we possibly measure and enumerate the victims of the cuts to social and health spending imposed by Harper ? Then there is the lost generation of young people , searching for secure jobs and meaningful futures that no longer exist in this dog-eat-dog neoliberal world . Every reader can add to the list . It goes without saying , in fairness , we would only count the victims since Harper came to power . A separate accounting is required for all previous prime ministers . <p> Harper has opened a political Pandora 's box . Let us talk about the victims of the system and let us assign blame to those who hold the power @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ worse . Harper 's changes have been for the worse . * <p> J. F.
